=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
A string is defined to be a valid parenthesis sequence if and only if it satisfies one of the following conditions:

- It is an empty string.
- There exists a valid parenthesis sequence A such that the string is obtained by concatenating (, A, and ) in this order.
- There exist non-empty valid parenthesis sequences A and B such that the string is obtained by concatenating A and B in this order.

You are given a valid parenthesis sequence S of length N. You can perform the following operation any number of times:

- Choose a contiguous substring of S that is a valid parenthesis sequence, and reverse it.

Here, reversing the substring of S from the l-th character to the r-th character means the following:

- For every integer i satisfying l \leq i \leq r, simultaneously replace S_i with ) if S_{l+r-i} is (, and with ( if S_{l+r-i} is ).(Note that reversing here is different from the usual definition of reversing.)

Find the number, modulo 998244353, of distinct strings S that you can have at the end of the process.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
S

Output

Print the answer.

Constraints


- 1 \leq N \leq 5000
- |S| = N
- S is a valid parenthesis sequence.

Sample Input 1

6
(())()

Sample Output 1

2

For example, you can transform S into ()(()) by doing the following:

- Choose the substring from the 1st to the 6th character of S. This is a valid parenthesis sequence. S becomes ()(()).

The only other string that can be formed is (())(). Thus, the answer is 2.

Sample Input 2

2
()

Sample Output 2

1

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python

```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

But wait, is the unordered tree truly invariant under operation? Let's rigorously prove. Operation on a valid substring (forest block) replaces it by rc(block). For a forest block T1...Tk, rc(block)=F(Tk)...F(T1). F(T) is the mirror image of T, which has the same unordered tree. The order of the block is reversed, but as a multiset of child subtrees at the parent, the multiset is unchanged (each selected child subtree replaced by its mirror, which is isomorphic as unordered tree; order reversed). So at every node, the multiset of child unordered subtrees is unchanged. Thus the unordered rooted tree (with children as multisets of unordered subtrees) is invariant. Good.

We can permute children at each node independently using adjacent swaps, but adjacent swap operation at parent swaps two adjacent children and mirrors both. Need construct sequence to reach target ordered tree.

Simpler: We can show the group generated by operations contains all transformations that permute children at any node arbitrarily and leave all subtrees unchanged (as ordered trees), by the adjacent swap with mirror compensation as above. Let's verify compensation sequence works for ordered subtrees:
Initial at parent: ... A, B ... (ordered subtrees).
Operation on block [A,B]: result ... F(B), F(A) ...
Then operation on single F(A) (currently second): result ... F(B), A ...
Then operation on single F(B) (currently first): result ... B, A ...
Thus swapped A and B, both restored exactly. This sequence uses operations at parent on block of two children and on single children. It does not affect other children. Therefore adjacent swap of any two adjacent children is possible while preserving all child subtrees exactly. Since adjacent swaps generate any permutation of the children at that node, we can reorder children at any node arbitrarily while preserving all child subtrees. This can be done independently for each node. Therefore any global assignment of child orders is reachable. Need be careful: The operations at parent on a child (single) are allowed only if the child substring is a valid parenthesis sequence, which it is. 1. block [A,B] -> [B', A']

Now, can we transform each child subtree to any plane embedding independently? We can use operations inside child. But note operations inside child are also operations at nodes within child; they don't affect siblings. So yes. To reach a target global plane embedding, we can:
1. For each node, transform its child subtrees to desired plane embeddings (bottom-up).
2. Permute children at each node to desired order using adjacent swaps (which may temporarily F-toggle children but restores them).
But if we permute after transforming, adjacent swaps restore child subtrees exactly, so order and embeddings preserved. Thus any combination reachable.

Now need compute this count for N up to 5000.

We need parse S into a rooted ordered tree. Since S is a valid parenthesis sequence, it may be a forest of top-level trees. We can add a virtual root whose children are the top-level components. Then answer is product over all nodes (including virtual root) of multinomial coefficients for its children's unordered isomorphism classes. The virtual root's children are top-level components; its degree can be up to N/2. The string S itself corresponds to the ordered forest; adding virtual root with parentheses? We don't need output string, just count. The operation on whole string corresponds to virtual root's children block. The empty? N>=1.

Need compute unordered isomorphism class for each node. For rooted unordered trees, two nodes have same type iff their children multisets of types are equal. We can compute a canonical ID for each node based on the multiset of child IDs. Since N<=5000, we can do this efficiently.

Approach:
- Parse S to build tree: For each matching pair, create node id. We need parent-child relationships. N up to 5000, so O(N^2) okay, O(N log N) fine.
- Use stack to parse: iterate chars. When char '(' push new node? But need know matching. Simpler: use stack of node ids. For each '(' create node with parent = stack[-1] (or virtual root), push. For ')' pop. This builds ordered tree. Node count M = number of '(' = N/2 <=2500. Virtual root id 0.
- Need children lists in original order for each node.

Now compute unordered type IDs bottom-up. Since parent appears before children? In DFS parse, a node's children are processed before the node closes. We can compute after building tree via postorder. For each node v, we have list of child IDs. We need assign an ID to the unordered rooted tree type of v. The type of v is determined by the multiset of types of its children. So we can compute a canonical representation string or tuple of sorted child type IDs. Since total children <= M-1 <=2500, we can for each node sort its child type IDs and use a dictionary mapping tuple to ID. Complexity O(total children log deg) = O(N log N). Need be careful: If two nodes have same multiset of child type IDs, they have same unordered type. This is a standard canonical form for unordered rooted trees (no labels). Since child type IDs are canonical for unordered subtrees, sorting them gives canonical representation. Good.

But wait: The type of a node as an unordered rooted tree includes the node itself and children as a multiset of child unordered types. The tuple of sorted child IDs uniquely determines it. Yes.

Then for each node v, we need multinomial coefficient for its children based on their unordered type IDs. We can compute from the sorted list of child type IDs: count multiplicities; factor = deg! / ∏ m_i! mod MOD. Multiply into answer. This is exactly product over nodes of multinomial. Note: The virtual root's type not needed, but its children's multinomial needed. We can include virtual root in the loop with children top-level nodes. Its degree may be 0? N>=1 so at least one.

Need precompute factorials up to M (or N) for multinomial.

Sample1 S="(())()" length6. Parse:

node1 child leaf node3? Actually "(())" = node with one leaf child. Let's assign:

node2 leaf.
node3 leaf.

leaf node3 type: children empty -> tuple () -> id1.
node1 children [id1] sorted [id1] -> type id2.
node2 leaf children empty -> id1.
virtual root children [node1 type id2, node2 type id1] sorted [id1,id2].

1. Read N, S.
2. Build tree:
   - M = N//2 maybe number of '(' = N/2. Since valid, N even. But N constraint says 1<=N<=5000, valid parenthesis sequence implies N even. Use N.
   - children = [[] for _ in range(M+1)] where 0 is virtual root.
   - stack = [0]
   - node_count = 0
   - For i, ch in enumerate(S):
       if ch == '(':
           node_count += 1
           v = node_count
           parent = stack[-1]
           children[parent].append(v)
           stack.append(v)
       else: # ')'
           stack.pop()
   After loop stack should be [0].
   This assigns node ids in preorder. children lists in original order.

Need ensure M = number of '(' = N//2. If N maybe 0? no.

3. Postorder traversal:
   Since node ids are preorder, a parent always has smaller id than child? In this construction, when we create child, id increases; parent id is smaller. So children ids > parent. Thus we can process nodes in decreasing id order to get postorder? For each node v, its children have larger ids, so if we iterate v from M down to 1, all children processed before v. Yes. Virtual root 0 processed last.
   We need compute type_id for each node 1..M. For virtual root, we don't need type_id but we need child type IDs sorted for multinomial. We can process virtual root separately or include with type_id 0.

4. Canonical type IDs:
   - type_id = [0]*(M+1)
   - mapping = {} # tuple sorted child type ids -> id
   - For v from M down to 1:
       child_types = sorted(type_id[c] for c in children[v])
       key = tuple(child_types)
       if key not in mapping:
           mapping[key] = len(mapping)+1
       type_id[v] = mapping[key]
   This assigns same ID to isomorphic unordered subtrees. Is tuple of sorted child type IDs sufficient? Yes, because type_id[c] already canonical for unordered child. For leaves key=().

   Need be careful: If two nodes have same multiset of child type IDs, they are isomorphic as unordered rooted trees. This is true by induction. No need to include node degree separately because tuple length is degree.

5. Compute answer:
   - Precompute fact[0..M] and invfact maybe. Since MOD prime, can compute factorial and inverse factorial using pow. Or compute multinomial directly: fact[deg] * ∏ invfact[mult] mod MOD.
   - For each node v from 0 to M (including virtual root):
       child_types = sorted(type_id[c] for c in children[v]) # for v=0, type_id for children computed.
       deg = len(child_types)
       ans = ans * fact[deg] % MOD
       Then count multiplicities in child_types and multiply by invfact[mult] for each group.
   - For leaves deg=0, fact[0]=1.
   - For virtual root, children top-level; type_id of top-level nodes computed.

Let's try to prove reachable all plane embeddings more constructively:
- Lemma 1: For any node, we can swap two adjacent children without changing any subtree. Sequence:
  Let children A,B. Operation on block AB -> F(B), F(A). Then operation on first child (F(B)) -> B, F(A). Then operation on second child (F(A)) -> B, A. This swaps A and B and restores them. This uses operations at the parent. Thus any permutation of children at a node is possible while preserving child subtrees.
- Lemma 2: For any node, we can replace its subtree by any plane embedding of the same unordered tree. Proof by induction on height: For a node, first recursively transform each child to desired plane embedding. Then use Lemma 1 to permute children to desired order. This yields desired embedding. Since operations inside child subtrees don't affect siblings, and Lemma 1 preserves child subtrees, the construction works.
- Lemma 3: The unordered tree is invariant. Therefore reachable strings are exactly plane embeddings.
This is solid.

Now, one subtle point: Lemma 1's sequence uses operation on "first child" and "second child" after block. Are these substrings valid? Yes, each child is a valid parenthesis sequence. The block AB is valid because concatenation of two valid sequences. Good.

Now, if we want to permute children at a node, adjacent swaps can be performed. But after swapping, the children are restored. The sequence:
Initial [A,B]
1. block [A,B] -> [F(B), F(A)]
2. single first -> [B, F(A)]
3. single second -> [B, A]
Yes. Let's verify with A,B arbitrary:
Step1: operation on block AB (valid forest) maps to F(B)F(A). Step2: choose substring F(B) (first child) and apply F -> F(F(B))=B. Step3: choose substring F(A) (second child) -> A. Final [B,A]. Great.

Thus any adjacent swap is possible. Adjacent swaps generate all permutations. So any ordering of the current children is reachable.

Now, if we transform a child subtree to desired plane embedding, then later adjacent swaps at parent preserve it. So induction works.

Then for each node:
```
from collections import Counter
ans=1
for v in range(0, M+1):
    ct = [type_id[c] for c in children[v]]
    ct.sort()
    deg = len(ct)
    ans = ans * fact[deg] % MOD
    i=0
    while i<deg:
        j=i
        while j<deg and ct[j]==ct[i]: j+=1
        m=j-i
        ans = ans * invfact[m] % MOD
        i=j
```
For v=0, type_id[0] not set but we don't use it. For v>=1, type_id computed. For leaves ct empty, ans*=1.

Now, let's think if there is any chance that the number of plane embeddings of the unordered tree is not equal to product of multinomials because the unordered tree may have symmetries that cause different plane embeddings to yield the same parenthesis string? We already considered. Let's formalize: A parenthesis string corresponds to an ordered rooted tree with no labels. The underlying unordered tree is obtained by forgetting the order of children at each node. Two ordered trees with the same underlying unordered tree are distinct as strings iff their sequences of child subtrees differ at some node. The product formula counts distinct ordered trees (isomorphism classes) with that underlying unordered tree. It is standard: For each node, the number of ways to order its children (which are unlabeled but have unordered types) is the multinomial. The choices at different nodes are independent. This yields the number of ordered trees. No overcount due to automorphisms because ordered trees have no nontrivial automorphisms preserving order? Wait an ordered rooted tree can have automorphisms if subtrees are identical? Example root with two identical leaves: ordered tree "()()" has an automorphism swapping the two leaves? But as an ordered tree, children are ordered; an automorphism of ordered rooted trees must preserve the order of children, so it cannot swap the first and second child because that would map position 1 to position 2, violating order. Usually ordered trees have no nontrivial automorphisms if we consider children as a sequence; the first child must map to first child, etc. Thus each ordered tree is counted once. Good.

Now, let's consider if the operation can produce a string whose unordered tree is the same but whose node "colors" (original identities) are permuted in a way that affects the multiset of child unordered types? No, unordered tree is an isomorphism class, not labeled. Our count uses isomorphism classes of child subtrees. The original node identities don't matter.

Now, let's think if there is an alternative interpretation: The operation "reverse substring" with parentheses swapped might not be exactly mirror image if the substring is valid but not necessarily a forest of siblings? We already covered.

[A,A] -> block [F(A),F(A)]; single first -> [A,F(A)]; single second -> [A,A]. Final same. Good.
If A and B arbitrary, sequence works.

Now, can we perform adjacent swap at a node if the node has only two children and they are the entire string? Yes, block is whole string, single child operations are valid substrings. Good.

In a general Dyck word, any balanced substring with nonnegative prefix is a concatenation of primitive components; each primitive component is a node. The substring's first char is '(' at some node; its matching ')' is included. The next primitive component starts after that node's matching ')'. This is the next sibling if the node is not the last child; if it is the last child, the next char is the parent's ')', which would be a closing parenthesis not starting a primitive component. So a valid substring cannot include the parent's closing parenthesis as part of a primitive component because a primitive component must start with '('; if it includes parent's ')', it would be unbalanced. Thus all components are siblings. Good.

Now, let's maybe write the code in a clean way:

```
import sys
MOD = 998244353

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    N = int(input())
    S = input().strip()
    M = N // 2
    children = [[] for _ in range(M+1)]
    stack = [0]
    node_count = 0
    for ch in S:
        if ch == '(':
            node_count += 1
            v = node_count
            parent = stack[-1]
            children[parent].append(v)
            stack.append(v)
        else:
            stack.pop()
    # factorials
    fact = [1]*(M+1)
    for i in range(1, M+1):
        fact[i] = fact[i-1]*i % MOD
    invfact = [1]*(M+1)
    invfact[M] = pow(fact[M], MOD-2, MOD)
    for i in range(M, 0, -1):
        invfact[i-1] = invfact[i]*i % MOD

    type_id = [0]*(M+1)
    mp = {}
    # postorder
    for v in range(M, 0, -1):
        key = tuple(sorted(type_id[c] for c in children[v]))
        if key not in mp:
            mp[key] = len(mp) + 1
        type_id[v] = mp[key]

    ans = 1
    for v in range(0, M+1):
        arr = [type_id[c] for c in children[v]]
        arr.sort()
        deg = len(arr)
        ans = ans * fact[deg] % MOD
        i = 0
        while i < deg:
            j = i
            while j < deg and arr[j] == arr[i]:
                j += 1
            ans = ans * invfact[j-i] % MOD
            i = j
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

Need check if for v=0, type_id[0] is 0 but children[v] are top-level nodes with type_id set. Good.

Now, let's maybe derive the formula for P(v) in terms of type_id to ensure no missing factor:
For each node v, let children types t_i. The number of distinct ordered trees with root v and given unordered children multiset is:
- Choose an ordered list of the child vertices (which are unlabeled but have types). The number of distinct type sequences is deg! / ∏ m_t!.
- For each child vertex of type t, choose a plane embedding of type t: P(t) choices. Since child vertices are unlabeled but positions in the type sequence are distinct, the total is deg! / ∏ m_t! * ∏ P(t)^{m_t}.
This equals P(v). If we multiply P(v) over all nodes, the P(t) factors for children are accounted by the nodes themselves. So product over nodes of multinomial only is correct. Let's verify with root U,U,L: P(root)=3 * P(U)^2. Product over nodes: root multinomial 3 * U node multinomial 2 * U node multinomial 2 * leaf 1 =12. Yes.

Now, let's think about if the operation on a valid substring that is a concatenation of primitive components where the components are siblings but not necessarily all children of the same parent? We argued they must be same parent. Let's prove by considering the first component's parent p. The substring starts at the opening of a child of p. For the substring to be valid, it must end at the closing of some child of p. If it tried to include a child of a different parent, it would have to cross p's closing parenthesis, which would make the substring have an unmatched ')' relative to its start. More formally, the height of the substring relative to its start is nonnegative and ends at 0. The first char is '(' at depth d+1 (where parent p at depth d). The substring ends at a ')' that brings the relative height to 0. This ')' must match the first '('? Actually in a valid parenthesis sequence, the last char is the matching ')' of the first primitive component if there is only one, or the matching ')' of the last primitive component. The matching ')' of the first '(' is at some position. The substring is a concatenation of complete primitives, so the first primitive ends at its matching ')'. The next primitive starts immediately after. All primitives are at the same depth d+1? Wait, if the first primitive is a child of p, its matching ')' is at depth d+1? The closing of a child returns to depth d. The next character after that closing is either the next child of p (depth d+1 opening) or p's closing (depth d-1). If it's p's closing, the substring would end? But if the substring continues with p's closing, that closing would decrease relative height to -1, invalid. So it cannot continue. Therefore the next primitive must be the next child of p. Thus all primitives are children of p. Good.

Now, let
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
to ensure no hidden issue: The operation on a valid substring is reverse-complement, which corresponds to mirroring a forest. The reachable strings are exactly all plane embeddings of the same unordered rooted tree. Thus answer is product over nodes of multinomial coefficients of their children's unordered subtree types.

```python
import sys

MOD = 998244353

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    N = int(input())
    S = input().strip()

    M = N // 2
    children = [[] for _ in range(M + 1)]

    stack = [0]
    node_count = 0
    for ch in S:
        if ch == '(':
            node_count += 1
            v = node_count
            parent = stack[-1]
            children[parent].append(v)
            stack.append(v)
        else:
            stack.pop()

    fact = [1] * (M + 1)
    for i in range(1, M + 1):
        fact[i] = fact[i - 1] * i % MOD

    invfact = [1] * (M + 1)
    invfact[M] = pow(fact[M], MOD - 2, MOD)
    for i in range(M, 0, -1):
        invfact[i - 1] = invfact[i] * i % MOD

    type_id = [0] * (M + 1)
    mp = {}

    for v in range(M, 0, -1):
        key = tuple(sorted(type_id[c] for c in children[v]))
        if key not in mp:
            mp[key] = len(mp) + 1
        type_id[v] = mp[key]

    ans = 1

    for v in range(M + 1):
        arr = [type_id[c] for c in children[v]]
        arr.sort()
        deg = len(arr)

        ans = ans * fact[deg] % MOD

        i = 0
        while i < deg:
            j = i
            while j < deg and arr[j] == arr[i]:
                j += 1
            ans = ans * invfact[j - i] % MOD
            i = j

    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
